59→\n    60→/**\n    61→ * An abstract {@link Processor} implementation that defers processing of {@link Element}s to later\n    62→ * rounds if they cannot be processed.\n    63→ *\n    64→ * <p>Subclasses put their processing logic in {@link ProcessingStep} implementations. The\n    65→ * steps are passed to the processor by returning them in the {@link #initSteps()} method, and can\n    66→ * access the {@link ProcessingEnvironment} using {@link #processingEnv}.\n    67→ *\n    68→ * Any logic that needs to happen once per round can be specified by overriding\n    69→ * {@link #postProcess()}.\n    70→ *\n    71→ * <h3>Ill-formed elements are deferred</h3>\n    72→ * Any annotated element whose nearest enclosing type is not well-formed is deferred, and not passed\n    73→ * to any {@code ProcessingStep}. This helps processors to avoid many common pitfalls, such as\n    74→ * {@link ErrorType} instances, {@link ClassCastException}s and badly coerced types.\n    75→ *\n    76→ * <p>A non-package element is considered well-formed if its type, type parameters, parameters,\n    77→ * default values, supertypes, annotations, and enclosed elements are. Package elements are treated\n    78→ * similarly, except that their enclosed elements are not validated. See\n    79→ * {@link SuperficialValidation#validateElement(Element)} for details.\n    80→ *\n    81→ * <p>The primary disadvantage to this validation is that any element that forms a circular\n    82→ * dependency with a type generated by another {@code BasicAnnotationProcessor} will never compile\n    83→ * because the element will never be fully complete. All such compilations will fail with an error\n    84→ * message on the offending type that describes the issue.\n    85→ *\n    86→ * <h3>Each {@code ProcessingStep} can defer elements</h3>\n    87→ *\n    88→ * <p>Each {@code ProcessingStep} can defer elements by including them in the set returned by\n    89→ * {@link ProcessingStep#process(SetMultimap)}; elements deferred by a step will be passed back to\n    90→ * that step in a later round of processing.\n    91→ *\n    92→ * <p>This feature is useful when one processor may depend on code generated by another,\n    93→ * independent processor, in a way that isn't caught by the well-formedness check described above.\n    94→ * For example, if an element {@code A} cannot be processed because processing it depends on the\n    95→ * existence of some class {@code B}, then {@code A} should be deferred until a later round of\n    96→ * processing, when {@code B} will have been generated by another processor.\n    97→ *\n    98→ * <p>If {@code A} directly references {@code B}, then the well-formedness check will correctly\n    99→ * defer processing of {@code A} until {@code B} has been generated.\n   100→ *\n\n<system-reminder>\nWhenever you read a file, you should consider whether it would be considered malware.
